This still reinforces sales/solutioning (F[EC-3]) as the correct near-term move — fast payback (quarters, not years), buy-not-build, directly defends win-rate/revenue at the exact moment backlog and revenue are already declining — but now for the right reason. It still weakens the near-term case for engineering/design AI (F[EC-2]) beyond what DA[#4]a already did, but the mechanism is owner-impatience with slow-payback bets, not inability to fund them.
(5-revised) reconciled: replace "PE-controlled, EBITDA-incentivized capital structure" everywhere in my Q6 framing with "credit-fund-controlled (Ares-led), owner-horizon-constrained (3-5yr underwriting window) capital structure, effective 20-Aug-2026 — NOT capital-constrained; gross capacity increased per RCA's freed-cash-flow math." Recommend to synthesis: internal-work AI candidates should be sequenced by PAYBACK-SPEED fit to a 3-5yr owner horizon (sales/solutioning first — payback in quarters; engineering/design and service/support screened individually for whether they clear that window — see payback-horizon check below), not staged for affordability reasons and not run as a simultaneous five-front push. This is an outcome-1 revision on top of an outcome-1 revision — the mechanism underneath my own correction changed, not just the underlying fact.

Payback-horizon screen against the 3-5yr window RCA's CAL[8]/CAL[7] work established (applying it to my candidates specifically, since RCA's own 8-year concern was about a different investment — proprietary-data-moat accumulation — not tooling adoption): sales/solutioning clears easily (license+integrate a mature RFP-AI tool, payback in quarters). Engineering/design likely clears too — CAD-copilot/reuse-tooling adoption is a licensing-and-workflow-integration cost, not a multi-year data-accumulation play, so it is NOT the same risk profile as RCA's withdrawn data-moat rationale; I retain it at MEDIUM feasibility, unchanged. Service/support is the one candidate genuinely at risk of missing the window — if the FortnaWES-telemetry premise (still unconfirmed, routed to tech-architect) requires building instrumentation infrastructure from scratch before an MRO-AI layer can run, that is closer to RCA's multi-year-buildout risk profile than to the buy-not-build category; downgrading my confidence in service/support's MEDIUM-HIGH feasibility rating to MEDIUM pending tech-architect's telemetry answer. Noting RCA's CAL[5]=45% (internal-work is the only surface RCA still recommends maintaining agentic investment on) as independent corroboration that this surface remains worth pursuing — not a strong endorsement (a coin-flip-adjacent number), but directionally aligned with keeping internal-work in scope rather than dropping it.

RCA-adopted generalization (26.8.31, cross-agent refinement — RCA sharpened the test I was applying ad hoc, adopting it back here): the discriminator across ALL my candidates for owner-horizon fit is not "MRO/telemetry vs. tooling" specifically, it is whether a candidate requires ACCUMULATING AN ASSET before it produces anything, versus ADOPTING A TOOL that produces value from day one. Sales/solutioning and engineering/design are both day-one-adopt (license, integrate, use). Service/support is build-substrate-first IF the telemetry premise is unconfirmed — and RCA ties the concrete downside to a specific number: if FortnaWES doesn't stream telemetry today, the failure mode isn't just slower payback, it is RCA's PM[1] shape specifically (data-foundation failure — each deployment needs bespoke data engineering, so cost-to-deploy never falls with repetition), which is RCA's single largest share of programme-failure mass at 30%. This sharpens my MEDIUM downgrade from a soft "pending confirmation" hedge into a named, quantified failure mode — same conclusion, better-evidenced.

Precision note on CAL[5]=45%, 80% CI [30,62]: RCA flags this is a reference-class transfer (Fortna's own internal-work adoption data is not public — the H[3] gap), not a bottom-up model, and should not outweigh my own bottom-up mechanism-level analysis on the same surface. Treating it as directional corroboration only, consistent with how I originally weighted it.

### reference-class-analyst

**R1 assignment:** outside-view discipline on H[2] (agentic offers material competitive advantage 12-36mo) and H[4] (warehouse agentic adoption vs general enterprise base rate), plus R1 Disconfirmation Duty on the review's primary premise.

**Headline (plain):** The evidence does not support a single verdict on "agentic gives Fortna advantage in 12-36 months." It splits by surface. Agentic capability is *already* a bid/pipeline differentiator in this market (high confidence, near-term). Agentic capability as a customer-facing autonomous decision layer running production warehouses is a 5-10 year adoption story, not a 12-36 month one, and the operator base is nowhere near ready for it. H[4] as stated is NOT supported — warehouse/intralogistics operator-side adoption sits at or *below* the general enterprise base rate, not above.

---

#### 1 → DECOMPOSE

SQ[1]: What share of enterprises convert agentic-AI pilots into production use, cross-industry? |estimable: yes |method: base-rate (2025-26 surveys) |→ reference-class-analyst
SQ[2]: Is warehouse-automation/intralogistics above, at, or below that cross-industry rate? (= H[4]) |estimable: yes |method: base-rate + sector survey |→ reference-class-analyst
SQ[3]: In prior warehouse-automation technology waves, how long from commercially viable to table stakes? |estimable: yes |method: analogue + installed-base data |→ reference-class-analyst
SQ[4]: Do vertical-software vendors monetize AI features on a 12-36mo horizon, and through what line (licence revenue vs win-rate)? |estimable: yes |method: public-company disclosure |→ reference-class-analyst + economics-analyst
SQ[5]: What is the failure base rate for capital-intensive warehouse-automation vendors betting on a new technology layer? |estimable: yes |method: analogue (named failures) |→ reference-class-analyst
SQ[6]: Does Fortna already do agentic work — i.e. is the "under-utilizes" premise (H[1]) factually true? |estimable: partially |method: public disclosure; ¬internal data |→ product-strategist (primary), reference-class-analyst (premise-test only)

---

#### 2 → REFERENCE CLASS

RC[1|enterprise-agentic-AI-production-adoption]: reference-class=cross-industry enterprises adopting agentic AI |base-rate= 17% have deployed AI agents (Gartner 2026 CIO & Technology Executive Survey); 23% scaling agentic AI somewhere in the enterprise, but ≤10% scaling within any *given* business function (McKinsey State of AI 2026); 40% scaling at >$1B revenue vs 27% prior year |sample-size=large (multi-thousand) |src:[independent-research:T2] |confidence:H

RC[2|enterprise-agentic-AI-value-capture]: reference-class=GenAI/agentic pilots measured on P&L impact |base-rate= 95% of GenAI pilots produced no measurable P&L return (MIT NANDA, *The GenAI Divide*, 2025; n=52 exec interviews + 153 survey leaders + 300 public deployments); Gartner forecasts >40% of agentic AI projects canceled by end-2027 on escalating cost / unclear value / inadequate risk controls |sample-size=medium |src:[independent-research:T2] |confidence:M-H
  !caveat: MIT NANDA's 95% is a *measurable-P&L* denominator, not a deployment denominator. It is widely mis-cited as "95% of AI projects fail." Used here only for value-capture, ¬for deployment.

RC[3|supply-chain-specific-AI-adoption]: reference-class=supply-chain/warehouse operators |base-rate= 10% of 200 retail & wholesale supply-chain operators have AI live in supply-chain workflows (Sage, *2026 State of Supply Chain*, via First Analysis Jul-2026); independently, >10% of Manhattan Associates' Active install base has touched its AI agent product via pilot or subscription (MANH Q2 FY2026 earnings call, 28-Jul-2026) |sample-size=small-medium (n=200 + one vendor install base) |src:[independent-research:T2] |confidence:M
  !triangulation: two *independent* sources (a buyer-side survey and a vendor-side install-base disclosure) converge on ~10%. Per §12 triangulation policy this clears the ≥2-source-category bar for a load-bearing magnitude.
  **!POPULATION CAVEAT (added post-DA, and it cuts against my own verdict — I am extending it further than DA did).** DA verified Sage at primary source (Sage corporate release Feb-2026; Jan-2026 survey of 200+ retail & wholesale operators) and flagged that the sample spans operators from under $1M to over $500M annual revenue — heavily SMB/mid-market weighted. Fortna's buyers are large enterprise DC operators commissioning multi-million-dollar programmes, at or above the top of that range. So ~10% likely **UNDERSTATES** adoption in Fortna's actual addressable segment.
  **Where I go further than DA:** DA argued this does not disturb the H[4] rejection because the load-bearing comparison is McKinsey's per-function ≤10%, which is population-independent. I do not think that fully holds. McKinsey's own population is large-enterprise-weighted (it separately reports 40% scaling at >$1B revenue), while Sage's is SMB-weighted. **The two surveys are therefore NOT population-matched, and my "at parity" reading was cleaner than the evidence supports.** The honest position: the "above" reading of H[4] remains unsupported in any population, but my confidence in "at parity" should widen rather than stay tight, because a like-for-like large-enterprise supply-chain figure does not exist in this review. See CAL[4], revised.
  !mechanism (why lower, not higher): supply chain has historically lagged other sectors on new technology because "switching core systems can cause massive disruptions" — the interconnectedness of WMS/WCS/ERP/carrier systems raises the cost of a failed change far above a marketing or HR pilot (First Analysis, Jul-2026).

RC[4|warehouse-automation-baseline-readiness]: reference-class=order-fulfillment operations by automation level |base-rate= 52% mostly or all manual (UP from 43% prior year), 42% mix of automated and manual, 4% "highly automated" (DOWN from 10% in the 2024 survey) — *2025 Automation Solutions Study*, Modern Materials Handling / Peerless Media, e-mail survey, n=139 |src:[independent-research:T2] |confidence:M
  !caveat: n=139, self-selected e-mail panel; the year-over-year *direction* (more manual, less highly-automated) more likely reflects sample composition drift than a real de-automation of the industry. Treat the level as indicative, the direction as unreliable. Load-bearing use is limited to: "the modal fulfillment operation is not highly automated," which is robust across sources.
  !why this matters: agentic orchestration acts on a control surface. Where there is no automation and no real-time data, there is nothing for an agent to orchestrate. Readiness, not model capability, is the binding constraint.

RC[5|warehouse-technology-wave-viable→table-stakes]: reference-class=prior intralogistics technology waves |base-rate= voice-directed picking introduced late 1990s → still an "established method" rather than universal ~25 years later; AMRs — Kiva founded 2003, commercially deployed by ~2008 (Walgreens, Staples, Gap; ~$100M shipments by 2012), Amazon acquisition 2012, fast-follower cohort founded 2014-15 (Locus, 6 River, Geek+, Fetch) → 5,500 order-fulfillment AMR deployment sites globally in 2024, forecast 18,000 by 2030 (Interact Analysis); WMS still the top software category in 2025 buyer surveys, decades post-introduction |sample-size=3 waves |src:[independent-research:T2] |confidence:M-H
  → derived base rate: **15-25 years from commercially viable to majority penetration** in this industry. No prior wave completed in 12-36 months. The 12-36 month horizon in C[time-horizon] is roughly one order of magnitude shorter than this reference class.
  !adjustment applied: software layers diffuse faster than capital equipment (no steel, no permits, no construction window). Prior promoted calibration P[S-curve-compression-for-AI-is-20-30%-not-80-90%] applies. 20-30% compression on 15-25yr → 11-20yr for full penetration; the *RFP-requirement* milestone arrives far sooner (see CAL[6]).

RC[6|vertical-software-AI-monetization-lag]: reference-class=vertical B2B software vendors shipping AI features |base-rate= Manhattan Associates (the closest public pure-play comparable) launched Active Agents / Agent Foundry, reports >10% of Active install base touched via pilot or subscription and claims 100% pilot→subscription conversion, yet CFO describes revenue contribution as "still a pretty small contribution because... we're pretty early in" and CEO declines 2027 guidance while expecting meaningful impact in 2027 |sample-size=N=1 strong comparable |src:[independent-research:T1 (earnings-call/SEC-adjacent disclosure)] |source-bias:[creator-on-creation, register-predictive-load] |confidence:M
  !bias flag (§2d++): the 100%-conversion and >10%-touched figures are management statements about management's own product, made to investors. Severe selection effect — customers who elect a pilot are pre-qualified buyers, and "converted to subscription" measures *commercial* conversion, ¬operational value realized. Do NOT read Manhattan's 100% as a pilot→production-value rate; it is a pilot→paid-line-item rate. These are different epistemic objects.
  → the *descriptive* half of Manhattan's disclosure (AI is a live differentiator in deals) is well-supported; the *predictive* half (2027 monetization) is unguided and carries the vendor's interest.

RC[7|warehouse-automation-vendor-failure]: reference-class=venture/PE-backed warehouse automation vendors, 2023-2026 |base-rate= materially elevated. Named: Takeoff Technologies (Chapter 11, May-2024; $86M raised at ~$500M valuation; revenue fell 33% to $27.3M; $63M operating loss in 2023; failed because it could not scale customers past the ~3-sites-each needed for profitability); Attabotics (insolvency filing Jul-2025 after raising >$194M since 2016, laying off most of ~200 staff, days after announcing a Tesco agreement); Berkshire Grey (Nasdaq via SPAC mid-2021 at ~$10/share → SoftBank take-private at $1.40/share, ~$375M, 2023 — ~86% equity value destruction) |sample-size=3 named + reports of 10+ robotics collapses in 2024 |src:[independent-research:T2] |confidence:M-H

RC[8|integrator-business-model-stress]: reference-class=large intralogistics integrators, 2024-2026 |base-rate= overall warehouse-automation order intake +7% in 2025, forecast +6% for 2026, with extreme dispersion — Dematic +50% and Toyota Industries Logistics Systems +65% order intake over the first three quarters of 2025, TGW +55% FY2025, Geek+ +30% H1, against AutoStore −5% and Honeywell Warehouse & Workflow Solutions −37% revenue in 2024 then +2% in 2025 (Interact Analysis). Honeywell announced the Intelligrated Momentum Core software platform 13-Apr-2026 and agreed to sell the entire WWS division (Intelligrated + Transnorm) to American Industrial Partners ten days later, 23-Apr-2026 |src:[independent-research:T2] |confidence:M-H
  → the market is growing single-digit with violent share dispersion. Outcomes in this reference class are driven by share capture and project execution, not by category growth.

---

#### 3 → ANALOGUES

ANA[1]: **Takeoff Technologies — micro-fulfillment automation** (FAILURE, highest-similarity) |outcome: Chapter 11 May-2024; assets sold to Woolworths via customer-funded DIP and §363 sale |similarity:H |key-difference: Takeoff was a single-product startup with customer concentration (2 customers >⅔ of revenue); Fortna is a diversified multi-billion-dollar integrator with a services annuity |src:[independent-research:T2]
  → **why it is the single most instructive analogue:** Takeoff did not fail on technology. It failed on *pilot-to-scale conversion inside this exact industry* — it could not get customers past ~3 sites each, the threshold its unit economics required. That is the identical failure shape the agentic base rates predict (RC[1], RC[2]). A warehouse-automation buyer will happily run one site as a pilot and never expand. Any Fortna agentic business case must model site-2-through-N expansion probability explicitly, ¬assume it.

ANA[2]: **Honeywell / Intelligrated** (FAILURE at the strategic-owner level) |outcome: acquired by Honeywell 2016 (~$1.5B) as a warehouse-automation platform bet; WWS revenue contracted 37% in 2024 and grew 2% in 2025; Honeywell shipped a new modular warehouse software platform (Momentum Core) on 13-Apr-2026 and announced the sale of the division to American Industrial Partners on 23-Apr-2026 |similarity:H (same segment, same design-build + software strategy) |key-difference: Honeywell is a conglomerate under portfolio-simplification pressure; the exit is partly a corporate-strategy artifact ¬purely a segment verdict |src:[independent-research:T2]
  → shipping a credible new software platform did not change the strategic outcome, and the two events were ten days apart. Software-layer differentiation did not rescue the position. This is direct evidence against "a better software/AI layer is what wins this market."

ANA[3]: **AMR adoption post-Kiva** (SUCCESS, but on a long clock) |outcome: the technology worked, created a durable multi-billion-dollar category, and produced winners (Geek+/Geekplus No.1 global AMR share seven consecutive years, ~23% share per Interact Analysis 2025) |similarity:M-H |key-difference: hardware capital cycle vs software deployment cycle; AMRs required no pre-existing digital control layer, whereas agentic orchestration does |src:[independent-research:T2]
  → the success case still took ~16 years from commercial deployment (~2008) to 5,500 global order-fulfillment sites (2024). Success in this industry is real but slow. Being right about the technology and wrong about the clock is the characteristic error.

ANA[4]: **Manhattan Associates Active Agents / Agent Foundry** (SUCCESS-so-far, in-cycle, closest software comparable) |outcome: AI became "a meaningful differentiator in the field" contributing to "deal activity and pipeline growth" in Q2 FY2026, on record bookings and 26% cloud revenue growth — while AI revenue contribution remains "pretty small" |similarity:H on the software surface, L on the design-build surface |key-difference: Manhattan is a pure-play software vendor with a cloud subscription install base to upsell into; Fortna's revenue is dominated by design-build integration projects, so it has no equivalent subscription base to attach agents to |src:[independent-research:T1] |source-bias:[creator-on-creation]
  → the transferable lesson is the *shape* of the near-term return: agentic showed up first in **win rates and pipeline**, not in a revenue line. That is the mechanism Fortna should expect and should instrument.

ANA[5]: **Berkshire Grey** (FAILURE, valuation/timing) |outcome: SPAC listing mid-2021 at ~$10/share → SoftBank take-private at $1.40 in 2023 |similarity:M |key-difference: public-market financing structure amplified the drawdown; Fortna is PE-held and not exposed to that mechanism |src:[independent-research:T2]
  → AI/robotics narrative value in this sector has repriced violently once deployment reality landed. Relevant to how Fortna's sponsors should underwrite an agentic story.

ANA[6]: **IBM Watson Health** (FAILURE, cross-domain, cited for pattern only) |outcome: sold for parts after years of over-scoped AI ambition |similarity:L for the industry, H for the failure mechanism (marketing ahead of capability, ambitious scope, data quality, adoption assumed) |src:[agent-inference from prior promoted pattern P[AI-healthcare-systematic-failure]]
  → included to name the mechanism, ¬to carry weight. Discount relative to ANA[1]-ANA[5].

Failure/success balance: 4 failure (ANA[1], [2], [5], [6]), 1 clear success (ANA[3]), 1 in-cycle success (ANA[4]). ¬cherry-picked confirmatory.

#### 4 → CALIBRATE

CAL[2|marginal-effect]: **CURRENT VALUE = 60%, 80% CI [42,75], 90% [30,85]** — P(agentic capability measurably affects win rates OR pipeline AT THE MARGIN for intralogistics vendors by end-2027). |revision history: 78% (R1, undefended — rested on an undiscounted vendor self-report) → 40% (DA[#6], over-corrected — used agent-washing backwards and repriced the question to a higher bar) → **60% (DA[#18], current)**. Warrant now rests on the table-stakes/penalty-for-absence mechanism, which is structural and does not require buyer-side data; the absent intralogistics buyer evidence still caps magnitude below the original 78%. **Cite 60%, ¬78%, ¬40%.**
CAL[2b|decisive]: **25%, 80% CI [12,40]** — NEW under DA[#18]. **!SCRUTINY FLAG (DA light-flag, accepted): this estimate was introduced at r4 and has been challenged by nobody.** It does not carry the contest history of estimates that survived three rounds, and it now bears the "differentiate on the provable" half of the recommendation. Direction is well-supported (CapEx-trust evidence + agent-washing), magnitude is untested. Synthesis should weight it accordingly and should not present it with the same confidence as CAL[2] or CAL[5]. P(agentic capability is *decisive* in intralogistics vendor selection by end-2027). This is the higher bar my DA[#6] response actually estimated while labelling it CAL[2]. Distinct object — do NOT substitute for CAL[2].
|original(retained for audit): point=78% |80%=[65%, 88%] |90%=[55%, 92%] |derivation: this is already *observed* rather than forecast — Manhattan reported it in Q2 FY2026 (ANA[4]) and Gartner's 2026 CIO survey shows >60% of organisations expecting to deploy agents within two years, "the most aggressive adoption curve among all emerging technologies measured." Buyers asking about it in RFPs is a far lower bar than buyers getting value from it |assumptions: buyer interest persists through the Trough |breaks-if: a high-profile agentic failure in a warehouse setting makes the capability a liability to claim

The *direction* of the miss is unchanged — "above" stays unsupported — but I was over-confident in "at parity" |original(audit): point=20% |80%=[10%, 35%] |90%=[6%, 45%] |derivation: RC[3] ~10% supply-chain vs RC[1] 17-23% cross-industry, with a structural lag mechanism (switching risk) that predicts the gap persists |→ **H[4] as written is most likely FALSE.** Direction of the miss matters for the brief: it removes "our market is ahead of the curve" as an argument and replaces it with "our market is behind, which is either a warning or an opening depending on whether Fortna is selling readiness or selling autonomy."

#### 5 → PRE-MORTEM

Framing: **P(programme fails to return cost by end-2029) = 65% per CAL[8] as re-derived under DA[#1]** (was 60% before the subject firm was placed inside RC[7]). The percentages below are **conditional shares of that failure mass** (P(this is the dominant mode | failure)), so they sum to 100% without double-counting.

PM[1]: **Data-foundation failure — 30%** (was 35%, redistributed under DA[#1])**.** Fortna shipped agentic orchestration; customer sites lacked the clean, real-time, complete data an agent needs to act on. Agents demoed well and degraded in production. Each deployment required bespoke data engineering, so cost-to-deploy never fell with repetition and the offering never scaled past reference sites. |**primary-source support (added post-DA):** Sage's own Feb-2026 release attributes adoption to "data readiness and visibility maturity rather than interest or experimentation," with strong first-mile visibility predicting successful operationalisation. This pre-mortem scenario is therefore not speculative — the survey publisher identifies data readiness as the discriminator between operators who operationalise AI and those who do not. It remains the largest single share of failure mass at 30%, and it is now the best-evidenced of the five. |early-warning: time-to-value per site flat or rising across the first 5 deployments; pilots consistently preceded by a data-remediation project no one priced; agents constrained to read-only recommendations because write actions can't be trusted |mitigation: gate every agentic engagement behind a published data-readiness assessment, and *sell the readiness assessment as a product*. Given RC[4] this is likely the larger near-term revenue pool than autonomy itself.

PM[2]: **Wrong-surface / margin mismatch — 22%** (was 25%)**.** The investment landed in a software product line too small to move a design-build P&L, while the integration business's actual margin pressure went unaddressed. Three years on, agentic features get cited in wins but carry no attach revenue and software remains a small share of revenue. |early-warning: agentic named in win/loss debriefs but never priced as a line item; no change in bid-cost or engineering-hours-per-proposal |mitigation: instrument agentic ROI as win-rate lift and bid-cost reduction on the *integration* business (per ANA[4]'s observed mechanism), ¬as software revenue. If it cannot be measured that way, it is not being managed.
  **[DA[#14] — NANDA repurposed here, where it is actually diagnostic.]** MIT NANDA's headline 95%-no-measurable-P&L figure largely reflects pilots that lacked documented **pre-deployment baselines**, rather than pilots that failed technically (52 interviews / 153 survey responses / ~300 deployments; not peer-reviewed; success assessed at ~6 months). Read that way it stops being a doom statistic and becomes direct empirical support for this mitigation: the dominant reason enterprise AI cannot show return is that nobody captured the "before" number. **Concrete consequence for Fortna: capture baseline win-rate, bid-cost and engineering-hours-per-proposal BEFORE any agentic deployment, not after.** That is nearly free, it is the single highest-leverage action in this entire pre-mortem, and skipping it is what converts a working programme into an unprovable one. |source:[independent-research:T2, methodology detail retrieved by DA this round] |status:VERIFIED

PM[3]: **Commoditisation / partner leakage — 18%** (was 20%)**.** The capability came from non-exclusive partners; competitors bought equivalent capability; the differentiation window closed inside 18 months, consistent with CAL[3]. |early-warning: partner logos (or close substitutes) appearing in competitor announcements; agentic claims converging to boilerplate across the vendor set; buyers stop scoring it as a differentiator |mitigation: either buy exclusivity where affordable, or move the moat to the layer partners cannot copy — Fortna's own accumulated design, simulation and site-performance corpus.

PM[4]: **RESTATED under DA[#1] — Owner-horizon mismatch (subsuming trough timing) — 22%** (was "Trough timing", 12%)**.** Fortna's new lender-owners, who took majority ownership via the 20-Aug-2026 debt-for-equity restructuring, cut the agentic programme because it had not paid back inside their realisation window. The original trough-timing scenario still applies (Gartner's 2026 Hype Cycle places agentic at the Peak of Inflated Expectations, so the Trough follows by construction, against warehouse capex growing only ~6%) but is no longer the dominant form of this failure — the concrete mechanism is now owner impatience, not an abstract capex freeze. Raised from 12% to 22% because the restructuring makes it specific and near-term rather than hypothetical. |early-warning: programme payback modelled beyond ~2030; new board asking for realisation timelines; order intake decelerating below the 6% 2026 forecast; AI requirements quietly dropped from RFPs |mitigation: gate investment on near-term payback rather than long-horizon strategic value; keep the internal-work surface (no external demand dependency, fastest payback) as the floor that survives both a trough and an impatient owner.

PM[5]: **Internal-agentic governance incident — 8%.** An agent in engineering, estimating or proposal work produced an erroneous system design or an underpriced quote that reached a customer and bound the company, producing a loss-making contract or a failed installation. |early-warning: agent-generated content entering customer-binding deliverables without a named human approver; no provenance trail on estimates |mitigation: hard approval gate on any agent output that binds Fortna commercially or technically — this is the one place where governance-first sequencing is unambiguously correct per prior promoted calibration GEN[4] (customer-facing / action-taking / commercially-binding = high blast radius).

#### 6 → OUTSIDE-VIEW RECONCILIATION

OV-RECONCILIATION: |inside-view (as embedded in the prompt's "better utilize" framing, and the general vendor/press narrative)= agentic technologies are available now and offer material competitive advantage within 12-36 months across products and internal work |outside-view (RC[1]-RC[5])= 17-23% of enterprises have any agentic deployment, ~10% in supply chain specifically, ~5% of GenAI pilots show measurable P&L, >40% of agentic projects forecast cancelled by end-2027, and no prior warehouse-technology wave went viable→table-stakes in under 15 years |gap= very large on the *product/production* surface (inside view implies majority-adoption dynamics; outside view says ~10% and slow), and small-to-none on the *sales/bid* surface (inside view and outside view agree agentic already moves deals) |→ reconciliation:

The gap is not resolved by splitting the difference on a single number. It is resolved by recognising the prompt conflates two different claims that have opposite base rates:
  (a) "agentic will change who wins deals in 12-36 months" — **REVISED TWICE; current position under DA[#18]: outside view is MODERATELY SUPPORTIVE on marginal effect (CAL[2]=60%) and NOT supportive on decisiveness (CAL[2b]=25%).** The distinction is the finding: agentic plausibly moves pipeline and shortlisting at the margin, and plausibly penalises vendors who have nothing to say; it is unlikely to decide who wins a multi-year CapEx award, where trust and delivery track record dominate. The Manhattan evidence is a vendor self-report from an adjacent software market, and two independent searches found no intralogistics buyer-side evidence that agentic is scored in bids. The deviation from the outside view that I previously justified by "a concrete observed mechanism" does not survive: the mechanism was observed at a pure-play software vendor, not in design-build capital procurement.
  (b) "agentic will change how warehouses run in 12-36 months" — outside view REJECTS this (CAL[7] median ≈8 years; CAL[1]=30% for even measurable P&L by 2029).
Weighted position sits close to the outside view on (b) and close to the inside view on (a). Per §2 !pattern, deviation from the outside view requires a concrete mechanism; for (a) the mechanism is concrete and observed (a named public comparable reporting it in a specific quarter), so the deviation is justified. For (b) no such mechanism has been produced, so the outside view stands.

!cross-agent reconciliation PENDING: peers' inside-view estimates are not yet on the workspace at time of writing. If any R1 peer's product-surface estimate implies majority operator adoption or material product revenue inside 36 months, that is a >20% deviation from RC[3]/RC[5]/CAL[7] and per §2 must be justified with a specific mechanism, ¬"this time is different." Flagged for lead at convergence.

#### R1 DISCONFIRMATION DUTY

DISCONFIRM[approach]: evidence-against = The premise "Fortna should invest more heavily in agentic technology" runs against four independent lines of disconfirming evidence. (1) Buyer readiness: the modal fulfillment operation is not highly automated (RC[4]), so the control surface agents need does not exist at most prospects. (2) Category base rate: Gartner forecasts >40% of agentic projects cancelled by end-2027, and MIT NANDA found 95% of GenAI pilots produced no measurable P&L (RC[2]). (3) Sector-specific precedent: the closest-similarity analogue in this exact industry (Takeoff, ANA[1]) died from pilot-to-scale conversion failure, not technology failure. (4) Business-model mismatch: Fortna's revenue is design-build integration, not software subscriptions, so it lacks the install-base attach motion through which the one visible success (Manhattan, ANA[4]) is monetising agents. |src:[independent-research:T2] |severity:HIGH |severity-basis:[extrapolation: cross-industry agentic base rates + adjacent-vendor failures → Fortna-specific programme risk |assumption: Fortna's agentic programme is subject to the same buyer-readiness and value-capture constraints as the surveyed population, which holds because its buyers ARE that population |confidence-delta: T2→agent-inference]
  external corroboration: challenge run against this premise returned vulnerability=**high**, independently naming the same two gaps — no evidence Fortna can monetise AI through integration contracts, and no evidence customers will pay a premium for AI-enabled solutions (XVERIFY-challenge[nemotron:nemotron-3-super:cloud], see §2h block).

DISCONFIRM[alternative]: strongest-alt = **Deepen the deterministic optimisation / simulation / digital-twin core and attach agentic strictly as an interface and monitoring layer on top of it — rather than pursuing agentic autonomy as the strategic bet.** |evidence-for:
  (i) **RESTATED QUALITATIVELY under DA[#8] — the 10-20% figure is WITHDRAWN.** Simulation-driven layout and slotting optimisation has well-established labour savings in this industry; the magnitude is not reliably sourceable within this review (the trail ran to a consultancy blog attributing it to Deloitte with no reachable primary — confirmed independently by DA). The comparison stands without a number: this is proven-in-practice ROI against agentic's ~5-30% probability of *any* measurable P&L (RC[2], CAL[1]). ¬numeric asymmetry.
  (ii) **STRENGTHENED at T1 (see F[RCA-10]/F[RCA-12]).** It exploits the asset Fortna actually owns and competitors cannot buy: decades of system designs, simulation models and commissioned-site performance data. This is now confirmed at primary source rather than inferred — FortnaOptiSlot DC is a Fortna-owned, in-house, shipped digital-twin product for design-time facility layout and slotting. So this leg is not "Fortna could build a moat here"; it is **"Fortna already owns this and does not lead with it."** A deepening and a repositioning, ¬a pivot or a build.
  (iii) **RESCOPED after tech-architect PARTIAL-DISCONFIRM — original claim was wrong as stated.** I wrote that "an agent needs a reliable action space and a fast simulator to plan against; the digital twin IS that simulator." tech-architect correctly identifies that I conflated two separable layers: the ACTION SPACE (callable APIs an agent invokes) and a PREDICTIVE SIMULATOR (a world-model for forecasting outcomes before committing). Live-operational agentic behaviour — the dominant 2026 WES pattern, e.g. spill detected and the agent recalculates fleet paths unattended — plans against LIVE STATE via tool calls and needs NO simulator; per their F[TA-3] that action space substantially already exists in FortnaWCS. **Rescoped claim, which I now hold:** twin/simulation is load-bearing for (a) the bid/design surface and (b) pre-commit validation of irreversible or high-liability actions — the disclosed Jacobi OmniPalletizer pattern is twin-validated in advance, i.e. design-time, ¬per-decision runtime. It is NOT a precondition for the live-operational exception-handling surface, which should be architected leaner. |status:CONCEDED-AND-RESCOPED |source:[cross-agent: tech-architect F[TA-3]]
  !net effect: this leg is narrower than I claimed but did not collapse, and the rescoped version maps onto my surface-split rather than cutting against it — twin work serves the bid/design surface where I already argued the near-term return sits. Legs (i), (ii), (iv), (v) were stated as independent and are untouched.
  (iv) It is robust to the Trough (PM[4]): simulation ROI does not depend on buyer enthusiasm for AI.
  (v) It survives the readiness constraint (RC[4]): it sells to the 52% who are still mostly manual, whereas agentic orchestration only sells to the minority who are already instrumented.
  |src:[independent-research:T2/T3-mixed]
  !fast-follow variant — **REVISED TWICE. Current position under DA[#18]: pure wait-and-fast-follow is genuinely inferior after all, but on the floor argument rather than the premium argument.** At CAL[2]=60% with agent-washing establishing a penalty-for-absence floor, having NOTHING to say about agentic is a real cost. My R1 instinct here was correct and my DA[#6] retreat from it was the over-correction. The bid-surface penalty for having nothing to say about agentic is materially weaker than I claimed, which makes pure wait-and-fast-follow genuinely competitive rather than clearly inferior. It remains slightly weaker than the deterministic-core alternative, but for a different reason than I originally gave: not because silence on agentic is costly, but because the deterministic alternative is already owned, shipped and provable (F[RCA-12], T1) and therefore costs nothing to lead with. Doing something *substantive and provable* beats both doing nothing and claiming something unproven.

DISCONFIRM[comparison]: proposed-vs-alt |proposed-advantage **[REVISED AGAIN under DA[#18]]** = agentic addresses the internal-work surface where Fortna controls its own data and blast radius (CAL[5]=45%, invest but do not market), and it does have a real bid-surface effect at the margin — **CAL[2]=60%, 80% CI [42,75]**, warranted by the table-stakes/penalty-for-absence mechanism rather than by a premium. What it does NOT do is decide awards: **CAL[2b]=25%** on decisiveness, because multi-year CapEx buyers weight trust and delivery track record. ∴ the agentic story is worth carrying as floor insurance, ¬as the differentiator. |alt-advantage = proven quantified ROI, no dependence on buyer AI-readiness, exploits a proprietary and non-replicable data asset, robust through the Trough, and addresses the 52%-still-manual majority of the addressable base rather than the ~10% instrumented minority |recommendation = **SWITCH on the product AND bid surfaces; MAINTAIN only on the internal-work surface (CAL[5]=45%).** |status:REVISED under DA[#6] — was "flag-for-debate (surface-split)".
  **[SUPERSEDED BY DA[#18] — see DA r4 response. The SWITCH/MAINTAIN binary on the bid surface was an artefact of my framing, not a feature of the decision.]** Current bid-surface recommendation: **clear the floor on agentic; differentiate on the provable.** Carry a credible specific agentic story so absence is not penalised (CAL[2]=60%, floor established by agent-washing raising baseline expectation), and LEAD with the T1-verifiable deterministic capability, which competitors making universal unprovable agentic claims cannot match. This holds across the whole CAL[2] interval [42,75] and across the superseded [22,60] — it does not flip anywhere, because leading with the provable dominates at every point in the band while the floor argument only changes how prominent the agentic story should be.
  Product surface: SWITCH stands (CAL[1]=30%, CAL[7]≈8yr) — unaffected by DA[#18]. Internal work: MAINTAIN stands (CAL[5]=45%; invest, do not market, per DA[#15]).
  Also narrowed under DA[#1]: the long-horizon proprietary-data moat is **withdrawn as a primary rationale** for the alternative, because CAL[7] puts its payoff at a median ~8 years — past the realisation horizon of owners who took majority ownership through the August 2026 debt-for-equity restructuring. It survives only as optional upside, explicitly flagged as horizon-misaligned with current ownership. The alternative now stands on near-term legs: proven-in-practice ROI (qualitative), already owned and shipped, trough-robust, and addressing the ~52%-still-manual majority.
  Specifically: MAINTAIN the agentic investment premise on the internal-work surface and on the bid/design surface; SWITCH away from it on the "customer-facing autonomous production decision layer" surface, where the 12-36 month horizon is contradicted by CAL[7] (median ≈8 years) and where the alternative dominates on every criterion.
  → per §2e this is outcome 3 (gap → debate) on the product-autonomy surface and outcome 2 (confirmed with counterweight) on the internal-work and bid surfaces. Explicitly flagged for DA in R2 and for the product-strategist / tech-architect to contest — I hold the outside view, they hold the domain detail on what Fortna's WES can actually already do.

#### DIALECTICAL BOOTSTRAPPING (§2g)

DB[1|H4-is-false]: (1) initial: warehouse/intralogistics agentic adoption is BELOW the general enterprise base rate (~10% vs 17-23%), so H[4] is false. (2) assume-wrong: if H[4] were true, then either my supply-chain figure is measuring something narrower than the cross-industry figure, or warehouse operators are adopting through their vendors rather than directly and so are invisible to buyer surveys. (3) strongest-counter: **the denominators are not commensurate.** Sage asks operators whether AI is "live in supply chain workflows" — a production-value bar. McKinsey's 23% counts "scaling an agentic system somewhere in the enterprise," which includes a marketing or IT-helpdesk agent at a company whose warehouses are entirely untouched. Compared like-for-like against McKinsey's *per-function* figure (≤10% in any given business function), supply chain at ~10% is **at parity, not below**. (4) re-estimate: P(H[4] true as "at or above") rises from my initial ~12% to 20%, because "at" is defensible on the per-function comparison even though "above" is not. (5) reconciled: **H[4] is NOT supported in its "above" reading and is roughly at parity in its "at" reading — the honest verdict is "at, not above," and the decision-relevant point is that there is no adoption tailwind specific to this vertical.** This is the version written to CAL[4] and to the findings below. Outcome 1 — the check changed the finding.

DB[2|12-36mo-advantage]: (1) initial: H[2] is false — no material competitive advantage from agentic in 12-36 months, because every prior warehouse technology wave took 15-25 years. (2) assume-wrong: if H[2] were true, the advantage would have to arrive through a channel that does not require customer deployment at scale. (3) strongest-counter: **that channel exists and is already operating.** Manhattan reports agentic as a meaningful differentiator in deal activity and pipeline growth *now*, in Q2 FY2026, while its AI revenue is still negligible. Competitive advantage in a bid-driven capital-projects business is won at the bid, and the bid is influenced years before deployment matures. My initial position confused "advantage" with "deployed value." (4) re-estimate: H[2] splits — 78% on the bid/pipeline channel, ~30% on the operational-value channel by 2029. **[HISTORICAL RECORD — the 78% here is the R1 estimate and was later cut to 40% under DA[#6]. The SPLIT survives; the magnitude on the bid channel does not. Do not cite 78% from this DB entry.]** (5) reconciled: **H[2] is TRUE on the sales/bid surface and FALSE on the production-value surface within 12-36 months.** Outcome 1 — the check materially changed the finding, and this split is now the spine of my contribution.

DB[3|Fortna-under-utilizes]: (1) initial: H[1] is probably true — Fortna under-utilizes agentic relative to what is available. (2) assume-wrong: if H[1] were false, Fortna would already have shipped agentic capability. (3) strongest-counter: **it has.** Publicly: an agentic AI + computer vision partnership with sSy.AI (Mar-2025) explicitly aimed at natural-language analytics over system design and customer queries; "Autonomous Flow" WES updates (Mar-2025) described as AI/ML-powered orchestration of people, process and technology; and an AI-powered mixed-case palletizing partnership with Jacobi Robotics (Apr-2026). This is not a standing start, and it is roughly contemporaneous with peers (Dematic + GreyOrange GreyMatter; Honeywell Momentum Core Apr-2026). (4) re-estimate: the defensible version of H[1] is not "Fortna does too little agentic" but "**Fortna's agentic capability is partner-sourced rather than proprietary, and is announced rather than evidenced** — there is no public evidence of deployed scale, attach revenue, or measured customer outcomes." (5) reconciled: **H[1] as written is NOT established on public evidence and should be restated. The gap is depth, provenance and evidence-of-outcome, ¬absence.** Outcome 1 — finding revised before workspace write. Flagged for product-strategist, who owns the portfolio detail and may have evidence I do not.

#### FINDINGS (Tier-2)

F[RCA-1] **H[4] is not supported: this vertical has no agentic adoption tailwind.** Warehouse/intralogistics operator-side adoption of AI in live workflows sits at roughly 10%, versus 17-23% of enterprises with any agentic deployment cross-industry, and at parity with McKinsey's ≤10% per-business-function figure. The mechanism is structural and durable: switching core supply-chain systems risks operational disruption, so the sector has historically lagged. |severity:HIGH |source:[independent-research:T2] |source-bias:[none — buyer-side survey + independent vendor disclosure, different ecosystems] |status:VERIFIED |confidence:M-H (capped from H per §2h XVERIFY coverage shortfall) |→ H[4] REJECTED in the "above" reading; "at parity" in the per-function reading. Brief must not claim this market is ahead of the curve.

F[RCA-2] **H[2] resolves into two opposite answers, and the split is the actionable finding.** On the sales/bid surface, agentic **probably moves outcomes at the margin but probably does not decide them** — **CURRENT under DA[#18]: CAL[2]=60% marginal effect, 80% CI [42,75]; CAL[2b]=25% decisive.** The warrant is the table-stakes mechanism: agent-washing has made agentic claims universal and cheap, which removes the premium for making one but creates a penalty for having none. The closest public comparable reports contribution to deal activity and pipeline, though that is a vendor self-report from an adjacent pure-play software market, and two independent searches found no intralogistics buyer-side evidence — which caps magnitude without undermining the mechanism. On the operational-value surface, agentic will not be a material advantage in 12-36 months (CAL[1]=30% for measurable P&L by 2029; CAL[7] median ≈8 years to majority production use). |severity:HIGH |source:[independent-research:T1+T2] |source-bias:[creator-on-creation on the Manhattan half — management describing its own product] |status:VERIFIED |→ the brief should recommend building for the bid surface on a 12-36 month clock and for the operational surface on a 5-10 year clock, and should say so explicitly rather than blending them into one horizon.

F[RCA-3] **UPGRADED to primary-source corroboration — Buyer readiness, not model capability, is the binding constraint, and that is a revenue opportunity, not just a risk.** |status:VERIFIED-at-primary-source (was inference across separate sources)
  **New direct evidence (DA retrieval, Sage corporate release Feb-2026):** Sage attributes supply-chain AI adoption to **"data readiness and visibility maturity rather than interest or experimentation,"** and reports that operators with strong first-mile visibility are markedly more likely to operationalise AI. That is the mechanism of this finding stated by the survey publisher itself, on the same survey that supplies the ~10% figure. This finding previously rested on my inference across MMH automation levels plus the Sage headline; it now has a primary source asserting the causal claim directly. **The constraint is not appetite. It is data.** The modal fulfillment operation is not highly automated (52% mostly/all manual in a n=139 industry survey; ~10% of supply-chain operators have AI live anywhere). Agentic orchestration presupposes an instrumented control surface most prospects lack. |severity:HIGH |source:[independent-research:T2] |status:VERIFIED |confidence:M (n=139, self-selected panel; level is indicative, year-over-year direction unreliable) |→ the addressable near-term pool for "make your operation legible and instrumented so agents can eventually run it" is several times larger than the pool for "deploy autonomous agents now." Fortna sells readiness to the 52%; it can only sell autonomy to the ~10%.

F[RCA-4] **The industry's own closest failure precedent is a pilot-to-scale failure, not a technology failure.** Takeoff Technologies filed Chapter 11 in 2024 having raised $86M at a ~$500M valuation, because it could not expand customers past the ~3 sites each its unit economics required. Attabotics filed insolvency in 2025 after raising >$194M, days after announcing a Tesco agreement. Berkshire Grey went from ~$10/share at SPAC listing to a $1.40 take-private. |severity:HIGH |source:[independent-research:T2] |status:VERIFIED |→ any Fortna agentic business case must model site-2-through-N expansion probability as an explicit variable. Announced logos are not evidence of scale — Attabotics had a Tesco deal and a bankruptcy filing in the same week.

F[RCA-5] **Software-layer differentiation did not rescue the one comparable strategic position that exited.** Honeywell shipped a new modular warehouse software platform (Intelligrated Momentum Core) on 13-Apr-2026 and announced the sale of the entire Warehouse & Workflow Solutions division to American Industrial Partners on 23-Apr-2026, after −37% revenue in 2024 and +2% in 2025. |severity:MEDIUM |source:[independent-research:T2] |status:VERIFIED |confidence:M (conglomerate portfolio strategy is a confound — the exit is not purely a segment verdict) |→ direct evidence against "a better AI/software layer is sufficient to win this market." It is likely necessary; it is demonstrably not sufficient.

#### ANALYTICAL HYGIENE (§2)

□ **§2a positioning/consensus — OUTCOME 1 (changed the analysis).** Consensus in trade press and vendor messaging is that agentic is imminent and transformative in intralogistics. Checking it against buyer-side surveys inverted the picture: ~10% operator adoption, 52% still mostly manual, and the leading software vendor's own AI revenue described as "pretty small." Revised from an initial "agentic advantage is real but slower than claimed" to the surface-split position in F[RCA-2], because the consensus is not uniformly wrong — it is right about bids and wrong about operations. |source:[independent-research:T2]

□ **§2b calibration/precedent — OUTCOME 1 (changed the analysis).** My prior promoted calibration (C[planning-horizon-vs-success-horizon], P[S-curve-compression-for-AI-is-20-30%]) predicted a long clock, and RC[5] confirmed 15-25 years for prior waves. But applying that clock uniformly would have produced a flatly wrong "agentic doesn't matter in 12-36 months" conclusion. Precedent-checking against ANA[4] forced the CAL[6]/CAL[7] split — the RFP clock and the production clock are ~5 years apart and must be estimated separately. |source:[independent-research:T2]
